**Shinzo Abe**[^cite_note-1] (21 September 1954 – 8 July 2022) was a Japanese statesman and politician who served as [Prime Minister of Japan](24833.md) and [President of the Liberal Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Liberal_Democratic_Party) (LDP) from 2006 to 2007 and again from 2012 to 2020. He was the [longest-serving prime minister in Japanese history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Japan_by_time_in_office), serving for nearly nine years.

Born in Tokyo, Abe was a member of the [Satō–Kishi–Abe family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sat%C5%8D%E2%80%93Kishi%E2%80%93Abe_family) as the son of LDP politician [Shintaro Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shintaro_Abe) and grandson of prime minister [Nobusuke Kishi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobusuke_Kishi). He graduated from [Seikei University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seikei_University) and also has attended the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_California), before working in industry and party posts, and was elected to the [House of Representatives](217778.md) in [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Japanese_general_election). Abe was LDP secretary-general from 2003 to 2004 and [Chief Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Cabinet_Secretary) under [Junichiro Koizumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junichiro_Koizumi) from 2005 to 2006, when he replaced Koizumi as prime minister. Abe became Japan's youngest post-war premier, and the first born after [World War II](32927.md). A staunch [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_Japan) and member of the ultranationalist organization [Nippon Kaigi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Kaigi), which holds [negationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negationist) views on Japanese history, Abe took right-wing stances including downplaying [Japanese atrocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes) in textbooks, denying government coercion in the recruitment of [comfort women](302767.md) during the war, and seeking revision of [Article 9 of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_9_of_the_Japanese_Constitution). In 2007, he initiated the [Quadrilateral Security Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue) with the US, Australia, and India, aimed at resisting [China's rise as a superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_as_a_potential_superpower). He resigned as premier that year due to his government's unpopularity and illness.

After recovering, Abe staged a political comeback in 2012, when he was again elected LDP president and led it to a landslide victory in [that year's election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Japanese_general_election). He became the first former prime minister to return to office since [Shigeru Yoshida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Yoshida) in 1948. Abe attempted to counter Japan's [economic stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lost_Decades) with "[Abenomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abenomics)", with mixed results. He was credited with reinstating the [Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership) with [a new agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_and_Progressive_Agreement_for_Trans-Pacific_Partnership) in 2018. In 2015, he passed the [Legislation for Peace and Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislation_for_Peace_and_Security) which allowed deployment of the [Japan Self-Defense Forces](2785204.md) overseas in certain strict conditions, which was controversial and met with protests by [Students Emergency Action for Liberal Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SEALDs). Abe led the LDP to victories in the [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Japanese_general_election) and [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Japanese_general_election) elections, becoming Japan's longest-serving prime minister. In 2020, he again resigned, citing a relapse of his illness, and was succeeded by [Yoshihide Suga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihide_Suga).

In 2022, [Abe was assassinated](71250084.md) in [Nara](6792846.md) while delivering a campaign speech for the [upper house elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Japanese_House_of_Councillors_election). The killer, [Tetsuya Yamagami](71250226.md), confessed he was motivated by Abe's ties with the [Unification Church](512933.md). This was the first assassination of a former Japanese prime minister [since 1936](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_26_incident). A polarizing figure in Japan, Abe was praised by his supporters for strengthening Japan's security and international stature, while opponents criticized him for nationalistic policies and [historical negationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_negationism), which they view as threatening Japanese [pacifism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacifism) and damaging relations with [China](13106128.md) and [South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_relations).

## Early life

### Family



The Abe family in 1956 (left to right): his mother [Yōko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C5%8Dko_Abe), Shinzo at the age of two, his father [Shintaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shintaro_Abe) and his elder brother Hironobu



Shinzo (center) with his family and grandfather [Nobusuke Kishi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobusuke_Kishi) (4th left), who influenced Abe's beliefs

Shinzo Abe was born on 21 September 1954 to [a prominent political family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abe_clan) in [Shinjuku](147734.md), Tokyo.[^cite_note-Death-2] His father [Shintaro Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shintaro_Abe) served in the [House of Representatives](217778.md) from 1958 to 1991, with stints as [Chief Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Cabinet_Secretary), [Minister for International Trade and Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_International_Trade_and_Industry), and [Minister for Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Foreign_Affairs_\(Japan\)), and was considered a strong candidate for prime minister before his death.[^cite_note-3] His mother [Yōko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C5%8Dko_Abe) was a noted [calligrapher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calligrapher).[^cite_note-4]

Abe's maternal grandfather, [Nobusuke Kishi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobusuke_Kishi), was a minister in the wartime cabinet of Prime Minister [Hideki Tojo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hideki_Tojo).[^cite_note-5][^cite_note-Kapur_25-6] At the end of the war, Kishi was imprisoned as a suspected ["Class-A" war criminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_A_war_criminal) by the [US military occupation of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Japan), but was released and later de-purged as part of the Occupation's "[reverse course](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reverse_course)" due to the [Cold War](325329.md).[^cite_note-Kapur_25-6] Kishi helped found the [Liberal Democratic Party](164231.md) (LDP) in 1955 and served as Prime Minister of Japan from 1957 until his 1960 resignation following the [Anpo protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anpo_protests).[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8] Abe viewed Kishi as his "No 1 role model" and was influenced by many of his beliefs, like Kishi's hawkish stance on China.[^cite_note-bbc_legacy-9][^cite_note-child-10] Regarding Kishi, Abe later wrote: "Some people used to point to my grandfather as a 'Class-A war criminal suspect,' and I felt strong repulsion. Because of that experience, I may have become emotionally attached to 'conservatism,' on the contrary".[^cite_note-child-10]

Abe's paternal grandfather, [Kan Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kan_Abe), was a [Yamaguchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamaguchi_Prefecture) landowner who served in the House of Representatives during World War II. Kan Abe was a pacifist who opposed the Tojo government and war in East Asia.[^cite_note-11]

### Education and early career

Abe attended [Seikei Elementary School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seikei_Elementary_School?action=edit\&redlink=1) and [Seikei Junior and Senior High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seikei_Junior_and_Senior_High_School?action=edit\&redlink=1) (成蹊中学校・高等学校).[^cite_note-12] Although as a boy he aspired to become a filmmaker, Abe's family history led him upon a political path.[^cite_note-economistlegacy-13] At the age of 17, he was diagnosed with [ulcerative colitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulcerative_colitis). He studied [public administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration) and graduated with a [bachelor's degree](188874.md) in [political science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_science) from [Seikei University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seikei_University) in 1977.[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16] From 1978 to 1979, Abe attended the [University of Southern California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_California) where he studied English as a visiting student. After taking courses in history, international relations, and political science for three semesters, Abe left.[^cite_note-17] Abe is reported to have been an average student.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202052-18]

In April 1979, Abe began working for [Kobe Steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kobe_Steel).[^cite_note-PROFILE-19] He left the company in 1982 to serve as secretary to his father, who had been appointed foreign minister in the [Nakasone cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Nakasone_cabinet). Shintaro Abe visited 81 countries in the 1980s. These travels imparted to Abe the importance of building relations with foreign leaders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202057–59-20] He continued as secretary when his father served as chairman of the LDP General Council and LDP secretary-general.[^cite_note-CHAIR-21]

## Early political career

### Member of the House of Representatives

After his father's death in 1991, Abe was elected to the [first district of Yamaguchi Prefecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamaguchi_1st_district_\(1947%E2%80%931993\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) in 1993, winning the most votes of the four Representatives elected in the [SNTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNTV) multi-member district. Abe joined the [Seiwa Seisaku Kenkyūkai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seiwa_Seisaku_Kenky%C5%ABkai), a [faction in the Liberal Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factions_in_the_Liberal_Democratic_Party_\(Japan\)).[^cite_note-22] Shintaro Abe had headed the faction from 1986 until his death. Yoshirō Mori and Junichiro Koizumi were seniors in the faction. When Abe first became a member of parliament, the opposition parties had agreed to form a "[non-LDP, non-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-LDP_and_non-JCP_coalition)" government with [Morihiro Hosokawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morihiro_Hosokawa) as Prime Minister. The LDP later returned to government by forming a coalition with the [Japan Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Socialist_Party), with JSP leader [Tomiichi Murayama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomiichi_Murayama) as Prime Minister. Abe and other conservatives supported the coalition, but criticized the compromises given, with Abe later wriing that "By making the leader of a party with public approval of only five or six percent prime minister, naturally the dissatisfaction within the party was amplified. The opposition was especially large from people who had become representatives who approved of the LDP’s ideas, who, if I had to say, belonged to the conservative mainstream."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202046–47-23]

Abe became one of the few newly elected LDP representatives to be named to the Foreign Affairs Committee. During this time, he extensively read on the constitution and national security, while also reading the writing of conservative author [Susumu Nishibe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susumu_Nishibe) and Spanish theorist [José Ortega y Gasset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Ortega_y_Gasset)’s *[The Revolt of the Masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Revolt_of_the_Masses)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202047-24] During the 1990s, Abe joined various right-wing conservative parliamentary groups such as the Diet members’ league of the Shinto Association of Spiritual Leadership, the 50th Anniversary of the End of the War established in 1994, and the Junior Parliamentarian Group to Consider Education and the Way Forward for Japan and the Nippon Kaigi Parliamentarian Association established in 1997. He was particularly active in the Junior Parliamentarian Group, where he served as the director of its Secretariat. Many of his future political allies came from these groups, including his close political friend [Shōichi Nakagawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sh%C5%8Dichi_Nakagawa), with whom he focused on battling the "masochistic" narrative on the [comfort women](302767.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202056–57-25]

Abe also took on the issue of [Japanese abductees taken to North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_abductees_taken_to_North_Korea); in 1997, he raised the issue in the Diet, where he criticized the government's response as "equivalent to abdicating the duties of the state". He became involved in the creation of the Association of the Families of Victims Kidnapped by North Korea and became the leader of the Parliamentarians’ League to Rescue Japanese Citizens Suspected of Being Abducted by North Korea. He attacked the LDP, the bureaucracy and the media for its handling of the issue, accusing North Korea of "state terrorism".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202060–61-26] In 1998, he became the deputy chairman of the LDP's Parliamentary Affairs Committee.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202067-27] Abe suffered a severe ulcerative colitis attack in 1998 but found a treatment to that allowed remission. April 1999 he formed a study group with [Yasuhisa Shiozaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasuhisa_Shiozaki), [Nobuteru Ishihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobuteru_Ishihara), and [Takumi Nemoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takumi_Nemoto) that was dubbed as the "NAIS Club".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202068-28] In 1999, Abe became Director of the LDP Social Affairs Division. In 2000, Abe's home and the office of his supporters in [Shimonoseki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shimonoseki), in Yamaguchi Prefecture, were attacked with [Molotov cocktails](20922.md) on numerous occasions. The perpetrators were several [yakuza](44774.md) belonging to the [Kudo-kai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kudo-kai), a [Kitakyushu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitakyushu)-based designated bōryokudan syndicate. The reason for the attacks was believed to be that Abe's local aide refused to give cash to a Shimonoseki real estate broker in return for supporting a Shimonoseki mayoral candidate in 1999.[^cite_note-29]

### Deputy Chief Cabinet Secretary



Memorial monument for Shinzō Abe at Osaka Gokoku Shrine in Osaka Japan

Abe was [Deputy Chief Cabinet Secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Chief_Cabinet_Secretary) in the [Yoshirō Mori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshir%C5%8D_Mori) and [Junichiro Koizumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junichiro_Koizumi) Cabinets from 2000 to 2003. During this time, he had a close relationship with Koizumi, though his relationship with Chief Cabinet Secretary [Yasuo Fukuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasuo_Fukuda) was chillier. In one instance in 2002, 2001, Abe secured a visa for former Taiwanese president [Lee Teng-hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Teng-hui) despite Fukuda's objections. In May 2002, Abe made an off-the-record statement during a speech at Waseda University stating that Japan was not prohibited from possessing nuclear weapons, which reportedly enraged Fukuda.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202086–87-30]

Abe was the chief [negotiator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negotiation) for the [Japanese government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_government) on behalf of the families of Japanese abductees taken to North Korea. As a part of the effort, he accompanied Koizumi to meet North Korean leader [Kim Jong Il](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Jong_Il) in 2002. After North Korea revealed a list of abducted Japanese citizens, with some being dead, Abe advised Koizumi to be prepared to leave without signing a joint consensus if Kim Jong Il was not prepared to apologize.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202093-31] He gained national popularity when he demanded that Japanese abductees visiting Japan remain in the country, in defiance of North Korea.[^cite_note-32] Kim later spoke to the Japanese delegation, acknowledging the "appalling incident", with the two countries subsequently signing the Pyongyang Declaration. North Korea's admittance of the abductions caused Abe's popularity to rise due to his previously hawkish stance on North Korea. Abe also successfully pushed Koizumi's administration for a tougher stance on North Korea. After North Korea allowed the five victims that it said were alive to return to Japan, on the condition that they return later to North Korea, Abe argued that they should not be returned, while Fukuda urged the government to honor the deal. Eventually, Abe secured a victory and the abductees were not returned; the eight family members of the abductees were eventually released in May 2004.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202094-33]

### Secretary-General of the LDP and Chief Cabinet Secretary



Abe (pictured in 2002) was first elected to the [House of Representatives](217778.md) in 1993.

In September 2003, Koizumi mulled appointing Abe as an LDP Deputy Secretary-General; instead he was appointed LDP Secretary-General.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202097-34] Abe was the leader of a project team within the LDP that conducted a survey on "excessive sexual education and gender-free education". Among the items to which this team raised objections were [anatomical dolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatomical_doll) and other curricular materials "not taking into consideration the age of children", school policies banning traditional boys' and girls' festivals, and mixed-gender [physical education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_education). The team sought to provide a contrast to the [Democratic Party of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Japan) (DPJ), which it alleged supported such policies.[^cite_note-SEXEDU-35] After the [2003 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2003_Japanese_general_election), in which the DPJ gained seats, Abe was demoted to acting secretary-general. Abe supported sending the [Japan Self-Defense Forces](2785204.md) (JSDF) on a dispatch to Iraq to help rebuild the country after the [United States invasion](201936.md). In a parliamentary vote to approve the dispatch of the SDF, Abe tried convince to three skeptical senior LDP representatives, [Kooichi Katoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koichi_Kato_\(politician,_born_1939\)), [Makoto Koga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makoto_Koga) and [Shizuka Kamei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shizuka_Kamei), to vote in favor, later issuing reprimands against them due to them abstaining.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020102-36]

Abe was quiet on the privatization of [Japan Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Post), instead focusing on cultural issues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020106–107-37] On 13 October 2005, Koizumi appointed Abe as Chief Cabinet Secretary, helping Abe to be more visible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020111-38] Regarding the economy, Abe favored fiscal expansion instead of raising taxes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020117-39] On 12 August 2006, he declared his candidacy for the [LDP presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020109-40] His chief competitors for the position were [Sadakazu Tanigaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadakazu_Tanigaki) and [Tarō Asō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar%C5%8D_As%C5%8D). Yasuo Fukuda was a leading early contender but ultimately chose not to run. Former prime minister Yoshirō Mori, to whose faction both Abe and Fukuda belonged, stated that the faction strongly leant toward Abe.[^cite_note-FACTION-41] On 20 September 2006, Abe was elected to succeed Koizumi as the president of the ruling Liberal Democratic Party.[^cite_note-ELECTED-42]

## First premiership (2006–2007)

Main article: [First premiership of Shinzo Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_premiership_of_Shinzo_Abe)

### First Cabinet

Main article: [First Abe cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Abe_cabinet)

See also: [2006 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election)



Abe (pictured in 2006) was the youngest prime minister since [Fumimaro Konoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumimaro_Konoe) in 1941.

On 26 September 2006, Abe was inaugurated as Japanese prime minister.[^cite_note-BBC-43] Elected at age 52, he was the youngest prime minister since [Fumimaro Konoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumimaro_Konoe) in 1941.[^cite_note-age-44] He was also the first prime minister born [after World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/After_World_War_II).[^cite_note-45] Abe's [first cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Abe_cabinet) was announced on 26 September 2006. The only minister retained in his position from the previous Koizumi cabinet was Foreign Minister Tarō Asō, who had been one of Abe's competitors for the LDP presidency. In addition to the cabinet positions existing under Koizumi, Abe created five new "advisor" positions. He reshuffled his cabinet on 27 August 2007.[^cite_note-46] Commentators noted that these changes seemed to be an effort by Abe to organize the prime minister's office into something more akin to the [White House](33057.md).[^cite_note-nytseven-47] *The New York Times* observed that his cabinet appeared to place a larger emphasis on foreign policy and national security instead of domestic concerns like economic policy. It also speculated that Abe's primary goal may have been to revise the pacifist constitution.[^cite_note-nytseven-47]

### Domestic policy

Abe expressed a general commitment to the reforms instituted by his predecessor, Junichiro Koizumi.[^cite_note-age-44] He took some steps toward balancing the Japanese budget, such as appointing a tax policy expert, [Kōji Omi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%8Dji_Omi), as minister of finance. Omi previously supported increases in the national [consumption tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumption_tax), although Abe distanced himself from this policy and sought to achieve much of his budget-balancing through spending cuts.[^cite_note-omiarticle-48] He started using the term "re-challenge" (再チャレンジ), emphasizing the need to give all Japanese second chances to succeed. Accordingly, Abe convened an advisory council to draft reforms to promote new opportunities for the disadvantaged. Lawmakers close to Abe, led by [Yoshihide Suga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihide_Suga), launched the Parliamentary League in Support of Re-Challenge.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020113–114-49] On 23 October 2006, Abe stated he was looking for some LDP members who were expelled for opposing Koizumi's privatization of Japan Post back to the party, which caused a decline in his popularity. Eventually, Abe sought a compromise by requiring those who were expelled that want to return to sign loyalty pledges.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris2020127-50]

In March 2007, Abe stated that there was no evidence that the [Japanese military had forced women into sexual slavery](173634.md#Japan_during_World_War_II) during World War II, which the Japanese government had admitted and apologized for in the 1992 [Kono Statement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kono_Statement).[^cite_note-Onishi2007-51] Responding to a potential motion by the US Congress encouraging Japan to acknowledge the atrocity, Abe stated that the Japanese government would not apologize again.[^cite_note-52] Abe held conservative views in the [Japanese imperial succession debate](3167373.md). Shortly after the birth of [Prince Hisahito of Akishino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Hisahito_of_Akishino), he abandoned a proposed legislative amendment to permit women to inherit the [Chrysanthemum Throne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrysanthemum_Throne).[^cite_note-53]

### Foreign policy

See also: [List of international prime ministerial trips made by Shinzo Abe § First premiership (2006–2007)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Shinzo_Abe#First_premiership_\(2006%E2%80%932007\))



Abe with United States president [George W. Bush](3414021.md) at the [G8 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/33rd_G8_summit) in [Heiligendamm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heiligendamm), Germany, 2007

Abe generally took a hard-line stance on [North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93North_Korea_relations), especially regarding the [North Korean abductions of Japanese citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Korean_abductions_of_Japanese_citizens).[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56] Abe publicly recognized the need for improved relations with [China](5405.md) and, along with Foreign Minister [Tarō Asō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar%C5%8D_As%C5%8D), sought an eventual summit meeting with [General Secretary of the Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Chinese_Communist_Party) and Chinese president [Hu Jintao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu_Jintao).[^cite_note-CNN-57] Abe also said that [China–Japan relations](13106128.md) should not continue to be based on "emotions".[^cite_note-EMOTION-58] Abe, in his four terms as the prime minister of Japan, sought to upgrade the strategic Japan–India relationship.[^cite_note-59] Abe initiated the [Quadrilateral Security Dialogue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadrilateral_Security_Dialogue) between Japan, the United States, Australia, and India in 2007, which was seen as a counter to China's rising power.[^cite_note-60]

### Defense

Abe sought to revise or broaden the interpretation of [Article 9 of the Japanese Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_9_of_the_Japanese_Constitution) in order to permit Japan to maintain [de jure](43594.md) military forces. He stated that "we are reaching the limit in narrowing down differences between Japan's security and the interpretation of our constitution".[^cite_note-newshour-61] During his first period as prime minister, he upgraded the Japan Defense Agency to [full ministry status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_\(Japan\)).[^cite_note-Japan_upgrades_its_defence_agency-62] Like his predecessors, he supported the Japanese alliance with the United States.[^cite_note-BBC-43] Abe supported the US-led [Iraq War](5043324.md).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64]

### Resignation

See also: [2007 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election)



Abe resigned as prime minister in September 2007 (pictured).

In the lead-up to the July election, Abe's Agriculture Minister [Toshikatsu Matsuoka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toshikatsu_Matsuoka) committed suicide following a series of political funding scandals. He was the first cabinet member to commit suicide since World War II.[^cite_note-65] Abe's ruling Liberal Democratic Party suffered great losses in the [upper house election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Japanese_House_of_Councillors_election), losing control for the first time in 52 years. Agricultural minister, [Norihiko Akagi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norihiko_Akagi), involved in a political funding scandal, resigned after the election. Additionally, Abe's rejection of a possible female Japanese monarch, which led to the Japanese succession controversy, diminished his support base.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] In an attempt to revive his administration, Abe announced a new cabinet in August 2007. Support for Abe rose by 10 percent as a result; however, the new agricultural minister [Takehiko Endo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takehiko_Endo), involved in a finance scandal, resigned only seven days later.[^cite_note-68]

On 12 September 2007, only three days after a new parliamentary session had begun, Abe announced his intention to resign his position as prime minister at an unscheduled press conference.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-70] The announcement came just minutes before opposition leaders were scheduled to question him in Parliament and shocked many. Abe had described himself as a "politician who fights" and previously pledged not to resign.[^cite_note-71] Abe explained that his unpopularity was hindering the passage of an anti-terrorism law, involving among other things Japan's continued military presence in [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghanistan%E2%80%93Japan_relations). Party officials also said the embattled prime minister was suffering from poor health.[^cite_note-72]

## Out of office (2007–2012)



Abe meeting with President [Ma Ying-jeou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Ying-jeou) during his 2010 visit to Taiwan

Abe remained in the National Diet following his resignation as prime minister. He was re-elected to his [Yamaguchi 4th district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamaguchi_4th_district) seat in the [2009 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Japanese_general_election), when the Liberal Democratic Party lost power to the DPJ.[^cite_note-73] On 15 October 2010, Abe delivered a speech in Washington DC to the [Hudson Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Institute) on [US-Japan relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US-Japan_relations). Japan had recently been surpassed by China as the number two economy in the world, a position that it had held for the previous 40 years. Abe said: "It appears that China hopes to gain control not only over Taiwan, but also over the [South China Sea](74209.md), the [East China Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_China_Sea) and, indeed, the entire Western Pacific... China's military strategy has rested on the concept of a 'strategic frontier'. In a nutshell, this very dangerous idea posits that borders and [exclusive economic zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zones) are determined by national power, and that as long as China's economy continues to grow, its sphere of influence will continue to expand. Some might associate this with the German concept of '[lebensraum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebensraum)'." Abe saw in the [ASEAN countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASEAN_countries) a counterbalance to Chinese expansionism. Abe feared the [Finlandization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finlandization) of Japan with respect to China, and saw the reaction of the [Kan Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kan_Cabinet) to the September [2010 Senkaku boat collision incident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Senkaku_boat_collision_incident) as "a very foolish move" and "frighteningly naive". He stated the "imperative that Japan conduct a review of its [Three Principles on Arms Exports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Principles_on_Arms_Exports)."[^cite_note-abe10t-74][^cite_note-abe10v-75]

While serving as a member of the Japanese Diet, Abe visited Taiwan in 2010 and 2011. There he met with president [Ma Ying-jeou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma_Ying-jeou), former president [Lee Teng-hui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Teng-hui) and future president [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen), who was then the leader of the [Democratic Progressive Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Progressive_Party).[^cite_note-Shu-ling2010-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] Ma described Abe as "the ROC's best friend" and said Abe was the third generation of his family to have close ties with the Republic of China.[^cite_note-Shu-ling2010-76] Abe also visited the [National Revolutionary Martyrs' Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Revolutionary_Martyrs'_Shrine), a shrine dedicated to the war dead of the Republic of China, including those who died in the Second Sino-Japanese War.[^cite_note-79] Abe later revealed that the illness that contributed to ending his first term as prime minister was ulcerative colitis, but that he recovered due to access to a drug, [Asacol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asacol), that was previously unavailable in Japan.[^cite_note-80]

### Second LDP presidency and 2012 general election

See also: [2012 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election) and [2012 Japanese general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Japanese_general_election)



Abe (pictured campaigning in 2012) briefly served as opposition leader.



Abe and other candidates campaigning during the LDP presidential election in 2012. His chief rival, [Shigeru Ishiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Ishiba), is standing immediately to his right.

Following the resignation of LDP president Sadakazu Tanigaki, Abe was re-elected as president of the party on 26 September 2012, coming in second out of five candidates in the first round of voting, but defeating former Defense Minister [Shigeru Ishiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shigeru_Ishiba) in a runoff vote by 108 votes to 89.[^cite_note-81]

Abe returned to the LDP leadership at a time of political turmoil, as the governing DPJ had lost its majority in the lower house due to party splits over nuclear policies and the cabinet's move to raise the [consumption tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumption_tax#Japan) from 5 to 10 percent. Prime Minister [Yoshihiko Noda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihiko_Noda) was forced to rely on the LDP to pass the consumption tax bill and in return was pressured by Abe and the opposition parties to hold a snap general election. Noda agreed to this on the conditions that the LDP passed a bond-financing bill and would support a commission to reform the social security system and address electoral malapportionment in the next diet session.[^cite_note-82]

On 16 November 2012, Prime Minister Noda announced the dissolution of the lower house and that the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Japanese_general_election) would be held on 16 December.[^cite_note-83] Abe campaigned using the slogan *"Nippon o Torimodosu"* (*"*&#x54;ake back Japa&#x6E;*"*), promising economic revival through monetary easing, higher public spending and the continued use of nuclear energy, and a tough line in territorial disputes.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85] In the elections on 16 December 2012, the LDP won 294 seats in the 480-seat House of Representatives. Together with the [New Komeito Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Komeito_Party) (which partnered with the LDP since the late 1990s), Abe was able to form a coalition government that controlled a two-thirds majority in the lower house, allowing it to override the upper house's veto.[^cite_note-86]

## Second premiership (2012–2020)

Main article: [Second premiership of Shinzo Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_premiership_of_Shinzo_Abe)

### Second Cabinet (2012–2014)

Main article: [Second Abe cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Abe_cabinet)

Emperor Akihito formally appointing Abe to office as prime minister in 2012

Emperor [Akihito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akihito) formally appoints Abe to office as prime minister, 2012



Official portrait, 2012

On 26 December 2012, Abe was formally elected as prime minister by the Diet, with the support of 328 out of 480 members of the House of Representatives. He and his [second cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Abe_cabinet) were sworn in later that day.[^cite_note-Shinoda_2013_230-87][^cite_note-88] The new government included LDP heavyweights such as former prime minister Tarō Asō as [deputy prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_Japan) and finance minister, [Yoshihide Suga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshihide_Suga) as chief cabinet secretary and [Akira Amari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Amari) as economy minister.[^cite_note-Shinoda_2013_230-87]

#### Economic policy

Main article: [Abenomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abenomics)

The Second Abe cabinet revived the Council on Economic and Fiscal Policy (CEFP) that had played a key role in formulating economic policy during the Koizumi cabinet, but had been abandoned by the 2009–2012 DPJ administrations.[^cite_note-The_Wall_Street_Journal-89]

Abe declared in his 2013 policy speech to the Diet that economic revival and escaping [deflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deflation) was "the greatest and urgent issue" facing Japan.[^cite_note-japan.kantei.go.jp-90] His economic strategy, referred to as [Abenomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abenomics), consisted of the so-called "three arrows" (an allusion to [an old Japanese story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C5%8Dri_Motonari#Legacy)) of policy. The first arrow was monetary expansion aimed at achieving a 2% inflation target, the second a flexible fiscal policy to act as an economic stimulus in the short term, then achieve a budget surplus, and the third a growth strategy focusing on structural reform and private sector investment to achieve long-term growth.[^cite_note-CSIS2013-91]

#### "First Arrow": Monetary policy



[Haruhiko Kuroda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haruhiko_Kuroda), whom Abe appointed as Governor of the [Bank of Japan](188155.md) (BOJ) in spring 2013, implemented the "first arrow" monetary policy.

At the first CEFP meeting in January 2013, Abe declared that the [Bank of Japan](188155.md) should follow a policy of monetary easing to achieve a target of 2 percent inflation. Abe maintained pressure on the bank's governor, [Masaaki Shirakawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masaaki_Shirakawa), who was reluctant to set specific targets, into agreeing to the policy. In February, after Abe publicly speculated that the government could legislate to strip the bank of independence, Shirakawa announced he was leaving office prematurely before his term expired.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93] Abe then appointed [Haruhiko Kuroda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haruhiko_Kuroda) as governor, who had previously advocated inflation targets, and who has pursued the government's policies of monetary easing.[^cite_note-94]

After the first meeting of the bank's monetary policy committee after he had taken office in April, Kuroda announced an aggressive program of easing intended to double the money supply and achieve the 2 percent inflation target at "the earliest possible time".[^cite_note-95] Over the first six months of the second Abe Cabinet, the Yen fell from a high of ¥77 to the dollar to ¥101.8, and the [Nikkei 225](40293.md) rose by 70 percent.[^cite_note-96]

#### "Second Arrow": Fiscal policy



Abe's Minister of Finance [Tarō Asō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar%C5%8D_As%C5%8D), who also served as deputy prime minister

The Abe Cabinet's first budget included a 10.3 trillion yen stimulus package, composed of public works spending, aid for small businesses and investment incentives, that aimed to increase growth by 2 percent.[^cite_note-97] The budget also increased defense spending and manpower while reducing foreign aid.[^cite_note-98]

In 2013, Abe decided to proceed with the first stage of the increase in the consumption tax from 5 to 8 percent in April 2014 (with a second stage envisaged raising it to 10 percent in October 2015). The bill to raise the tax had been passed under the previous DPJ government, but the final decision lay with the prime minister. While this was expected to affect economic growth in the quarter following the rise, Abe also announced a 5-trillion yen stimulus package that aimed to mitigate any effects on economic revival.[^cite_note-99] After the increase in April, Japan fell into recession during the second and third quarters of 2014, leading to Abe delaying the second stage of the tax rise until April 2017 and calling a [snap election](462915.md).[^cite_note-BBC20141117-100] In response to the recession, Aso announced that the government would ask the Diet to pass a supplementary budget to fund a further stimulus package worth 2–3 trillion yen.[^cite_note-101]

There had been some division within the Abe cabinet between "fiscal hawks", such as Finance Minister Aso, who favored fiscal consolidation through spending cuts and tax increases, and deflationists, such as Abe himself, who argued in favor of a "growth first" policy that prioritizes economic expansion and recovery over budget considerations using the slogan "no fiscal health without economic revitalization".[^cite_note-Abe's_Growth-First_Fiscal_Policy-102][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-104] Abe's decision to delay the consumption tax increase in November 2014 and his push for a large fiscal deficit in the 2015 budget without social security cuts was interpreted as a victory for this faction within the LDP. The government did, however, commit to a primary surplus by 2020, and pledged to review its strategy in 2018 if the primary deficit had not fallen to 1 percent of GDP by that time.[^cite_note-Abe's_Growth-First_Fiscal_Policy-102]

#### "Third Arrow": Growth strategy and structural reform

On 15 March 2013, Abe announced that Japan was entering negotiations to join the [Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Pacific_Partnership) (TPP). This was interpreted by analysts as a means through which the government could enact reforms to liberalize certain sectors of the Japanese economy, most notably agriculture, and was criticized by farm lobbies and some sections of the LDP.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] Economist Yoshizaki Tatsuhiko described the TPP as having the potential to act as the "linchpin of Abe's economic revitalization strategy" by making Japan more competitive through free trade.[^cite_note-107] In February 2015, the Abe government struck a deal to limit the power of the [JA-Zenchu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JA-Zenchu) body to supervise and audit Japan's agricultural co-operatives, in a move designed to facilitate TPP negotiations, improve the competitiveness of Japan's farming sector and curtail the influence of the agriculture lobby.[^cite_note-108]

Abe revealed the first measures related to the "third arrow" policies in June 2013, which included plans to establish [deregulated economic zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_economic_zone) and allow the sale of drugs online, but did not include substantial measures related to the labor market or business reform.[^cite_note-109] These measures were less well-received than the first two arrows had been since Abe took office, with the stock market falling slightly and critics arguing that they lacked detail; *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*, for example, judged the announcement a "misfire".[^cite_note-110] Analysts did note, however, that Abe was waiting until after the July [Upper House](217779.md) elections to reveal further details, to avoid an adverse reaction by voters to potentially unpopular reforms.[^cite_note-111] At the annual meeting of the [World Economic Forum](172185.md) (WEF) in [Davos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davos) in 2014, Abe announced that he was ready to act as a "[drill bit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drill_bit)" to break through the rock of vested interests and "red tape" to achieve structural reforms of the economy. He cited reforms in agriculture, energy and health sectors as evidence of this, and pledged to push forward with the TPP, a Japan–EU trade deal and tax, corporate governance and planning reforms.[^cite_note-112]

Abe announced a package of structural reforms in June 2014, that *The Economist* described as "less a single arrow than a 1,000-strong bundle" and compared favorably to the 2013 announcement. These new measures included corporate governance reform, the easing of restrictions on hiring foreign staff in special economic zones, liberalizing the health sector, and measures to help foreign and local entrepreneurs.[^cite_note-113] The plans also included a cut in corporation tax to below 30 percent, an expansion of childcare to encourage women to join the workforce, and the loosening of restrictions on overtime.[^cite_note-114] In December 2015, the government announced corporation tax would be reduced to 29.97 percent in 2016, bringing the cut forward one year ahead of schedule.[^cite_note-115]



[Akira Amari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akira_Amari), who served as Abe's economy minister from 2012 to 2016, oversaw the "third arrow" growth strategy and negotiations to join the Trans-Pacific Partnership agreement.

In September 2013, Abe called for a "society in which all women can shine", setting a target that 30 percent of leadership positions should be held by women by 2020. Abe cited the "womenomics" ideas of [Kathy Matsui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathy_Matsui) that greater participation by women in the workforce, which is relatively low in Japan, especially in leadership roles, could improve Japan's GDP and potentially fertility rates, despite declining population figures. The Abe cabinet introduced measures to expand childcare and legislation to force public and private organizations to publish data on the number of women they employ and what positions they hold.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]

In November 2013, the Abe cabinet passed a bill to liberalize Japan's electricity market by abolishing price controls, breaking up regional monopolies, and separating power transmission from generation by creating a national grid company. This move was partly in response to the [2011 Fukushima disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fukushima_Daiichi_nuclear_disaster), and the bill faced little opposition in the Diet.[^cite_note-119] By March 2015, more than 500 companies had applied to the [Economy Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Economy,_Trade_and_Industry) to enter the electricity retail market and the electricity industry was expected to be fully liberalized by 2016, with gas utilities expected to follow suit by 2017.[^cite_note-120] Abe had also said he favored the rebuilding of Japan's nuclear reactors following the Fukushima disaster (though much of the authority to restart nuclear plants lies with local governments) and planned to strengthen relations with the United States.[^cite_note-121]

#### 2013 Upper House election

When Abe returned to office, although neither party had controlled the [House of Councillors](217779.md) (the upper house of the Diet) since the 2007 election, the opposition DPJ was the largest party. The governing coalition enjoyed a two-thirds majority in the lower house, allowing it to override the upper house's veto, but this requires a delay of 90 days. This situation, known as the "Twisted Diet", had contributed to political gridlock and the "revolving door" of prime ministers since 2007.[^cite_note-122] Abe's campaign for the 2013 election focused on themes of economic revival, asking voters to give him a stable mandate in both houses to pursue reforms, and took a more moderate tone on defense and constitutional matters.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124]

In the [2013 upper house election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_Japanese_House_of_Councillors_election), the LDP emerged as the largest party with 115 seats (a gain of 31) and the Komeito with 20 (a gain of 1), giving Abe's coalition control of both houses of the Diet, but not the two-thirds majority in the upper house that would allow for constitutional revision.[^cite_note-125] With no national elections due until 2016, this result was described as giving Abe the opportunity of "three golden years" of parliamentary stability in which to implement his policies.[^cite_note-126]

#### Domestic policy

Abe's return to the prime ministership saw a renewed attempt to downplay Japan's wartime atrocities in school textbooks, an issue that had contributed to his earlier downfall.[^cite_note-127] In 2013, Abe supported the creation of the [Top Global University Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_Global_University_Project) program. This is a ten-year program to increase international student attendance in Japanese universities and hire more foreign faculty. There is also funding for selected universities to create English-only undergraduate programs.[^cite_note-QS-128][^cite_note-EAF-129] In 2014, Abe allocated millions of dollars of the fiscal budget to help programs that help single individuals in Japan find potential mates. These programs entitled "Marriage support programs" were started in hopes of [raising Japan's declining birthrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_policy_in_Japan), which was half of what it was six decades prior.[^cite_note-130]

#### Foreign policy

See also: [List of international prime ministerial trips made by Shinzo Abe § Second premiership (2012–2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_international_prime_ministerial_trips_made_by_Shinzo_Abe#Second_premiership_\(2012%E2%80%932020\))



Abe with United States vice president [Joe Biden](145422.md) in Tokyo, 2013



Abe with Brazilian president [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) in Brasília, 2014



Abe with Singaporean prime minister [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong) at [the Istana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Istana) in Singapore, 2014

Shortly after taking office Abe signaled a "drastic reshaping" of foreign policy and promised to pursue diplomacy with a global, rather than a regional or bilateral outlook based on "the fundamental values of freedom, democracy, basic human rights, and the rule of law".[^cite_note-japan.kantei.go.jp-90] His choice of [Fumio Kishida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fumio_Kishida) as [foreign minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Minister_of_Japan) was interpreted as a sign that he would pursue a more moderate line compared to his hawkish stance in the run-up to the general election.[^cite_note-The_Wall_Street_Journal-89] His first visit overseas after becoming prime minister once again was to various countries in Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-131] Abe increased its allies in its international campaign to counter a North Korean nuclear threat. Abe often visited countries such as Singapore, Japan's largest Asian investor and vice-versa.[^cite_note-132]

Within weeks of returning to power, the Second Abe cabinet faced the [In Amenas hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_Amenas_hostage_crisis) of 2013 in which 10 Japanese citizens were killed.[^cite_note-133] Abe believed that this incident demonstrated the need for the creation of a [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Council_\(Japan\)), and convened a panel to consider its creation soon after the crisis.[^cite_note-134]

Abe was unusually active in the field of foreign affairs for a Japanese prime minister, making visits to 49 countries between December 2012 and September 2014, a number that was described as "unprecedented" (by contrast, his immediate two predecessors [Naoto Kan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naoto_Kan) and Yoshihiko Noda visited a combined total of 18 countries between June 2010 and December 2012).[^cite_note-135] This was interpreted as a means to offset poor relations with China and the Koreas by increasing Japan's profile on the world stage and improving bilateral ties with other countries in the region. Southeast Asian nations, Australia, and India were significant and frequent destinations for Abe, who visited all 10 [ASEAN](106539.md) countries in his first year in office. The diplomatic tours also functioned as another element of Abenomics by promoting Japan to the international business community and opening up avenues for trade, energy, and defense procurement deals (for example, business executives often travel with Abe on these visits).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

In September 2013, Abe intervened to aid [Tokyo's bid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_bid_for_the_2020_Summer_Olympics) to host the [2020 Summer Olympic and Paralympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Summer_Olympics), giving a speech in English at the [IOC session in Buenos Aires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/125th_IOC_Session), in which he extolled the role of sport in Japan and sought to reassure the committee that any ongoing issues with the [Fukushima plant](11986472.md) were under control.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] After the bid was successful, Abe sought to portray the games as symbolic of his Abenomics economic revitalization programme, saying, "I want to make the Olympics a trigger for sweeping away 15 years of deflation and economic decline."[^cite_note-140]

Abe's foreign policy moved Japan away from its traditional focus on the "big three" bilateral relationships with the United States, China, and South Korea, and sought to increase Japan's international profile by expanding ties with [NATO](21133.md), the [European Union](9317.md), and other organizations beyond the Asia-Pacific region.[^cite_note-The_ASAN_Forum-141][^cite_note-cucek15-142] Abe concluded the [Japan–Australia Economic Partnership Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Australia_Economic_Partnership_Agreement) with Australia's [Abbott government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbott_government) in 2014 and addressed a joint sitting of the [Australian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Parliament) in July.[^cite_note-143] He was the first Japanese PM to address the Australian parliament.[^cite_note-144] In January 2014, Abe became the first Japanese leader to attend India's [Republic Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Day_Parade) in Delhi as [chief guest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_state_visits_to_India#Republic_Day_Parade_Guests), during a three-day visit where he and Prime Minister [Manmohan Singh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manmohan_Singh) agreed to increase co-operation over economic, defense and security issues and signed trade agreements related to energy, tourism and telecoms.[^cite_note-145]

Relations between Japan and its immediate neighbors, China and South Korea, remained poor after Abe's return to office. While he declared that the "doors are always open on my side", no bilateral meetings between Abe and Chinese leadership took place for the first 23 months of his second term.[^cite_note-CSIS2013-91][^cite_note-TJT-146] Neither did Abe hold any meetings with President [Park Geun-hye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Geun-hye) of South Korea during his 2012 to 2014 term of office.[^cite_note-The_Guardian-147] Both countries criticized Abe's [visit to the Yasukuni Shrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controversies_surrounding_Yasukuni_Shrine#Shinzo_Abe) in December 2013, with the Chinese foreign minister describing the action as moving Japan in an "extremely dangerous" direction.[^cite_note-148] In addition China continued to criticize Abe's defense reform policies, warning that Japan should not abandon its post-war policy of pacifism.[^cite_note-149] Abe's speech at the WEF in 2014 was interpreted as a criticism of Chinese foreign and defense policy when he said that "the dividends of growth in Asia must not be wasted on military expansion" and called for greater preservation of the [freedom of the seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_seas) under the [rule of law](25166191.md), although he did not specifically refer to any one country during his remarks.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151] In November 2014, Abe met Chinese leader [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) at the [APEC meeting in Beijing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC_China_2014) for the first time since either had taken office, after a photocall that was described as "awkward" by the press. Abe later told reporters that during the meeting he suggested establishing a hotline between Tokyo and Beijing to help resolve any maritime clashes and that the "first step" had been taken to improve relations.[^cite_note-TJT-146][^cite_note-152]

#### Defense and security policy

Abe tried to centralize security policy in the prime minister's office by creating the [National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Security_Council_\(Japan\)) to better coordinate national security policy, and by ordering the first National Security Strategy in Japan's history.[^cite_note-153] Based upon the [American body of the same name](44026.md), the law to create the NSC was passed in November 2013 and began operating the following month when Abe appointed [Shotaro Yachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shotaro_Yachi) as Japan's first National Security Advisor.[^cite_note-154] In December 2013, Abe announced a five-year plan of military expansion. He described this as "proactive pacificism", with the goal of making Japan a more "normal" country, able to defend itself.[^cite_note-155]

In the same month, the Diet passed the Abe cabinet's [State Secrecy Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Secrecy_Law), which took effect in December 2014.[^cite_note-156] The law expanded the scope for the government to designate what information constitutes a state secret and increased penalties for bureaucrats and journalists who leak such information to up to 10 years in prison and a 10-million-yen fine. The passage of the law proved controversial, with thousands protesting the bill in Tokyo and the cabinet's approval rating falling below 50 percent for the first time in some polls. Detractors argued that the law was ambiguous and therefore gave the government too much freedom to decide which information to classify, that it could curtail freedom of the press, and that the cabinet had rushed the legislation without including any corresponding freedom of information guarantees.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] Abe argued that the law was necessary and applied only in cases of national security, diplomacy, public safety and counter-terrorism, saying, "If the law prevents films from being made, or weakens freedom of the press, I'll resign."[^cite_note-159] However he did concede that, in retrospect, the government should have explained the details of the bill more carefully to the public.[^cite_note-160]

In July 2014, the Abe cabinet decided to reinterpret [Japan's constitution](62699.md) to allow for the right of "Collective Self-Defense".[^cite_note-161] This would allow the [Self Defense Forces](2785204.md) to come to the aid of, and defend, an ally under attack, whereas the previous interpretation of the constitution was strictly pacifist and allowed for the force to be used only in absolute self-defence.[^cite_note-162] The decision was supported by the United States, which has argued for greater scope for action by Japan as a regional ally, and led to a revision of the US-Japan defense cooperation guidelines in 2015.[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164]

#### 2014 cabinet reshuffle

The cabinet inaugurated in December 2012 was the longest-serving and most stable in post-war Japanese history, lasting 617 days without a change in personnel until Abe conducted a reshuffle in September 2014, with the stated aim of promoting more women into ministerial posts. The reshuffled cabinet tied the record of five women ministers set by the first Koizumi cabinet. Most key figures, such as Deputy Prime Minister Aso and Chief Cabinet Secretary Suga, were kept in their posts although Abe moved Justice Minister Sadakazu Tanigaki out of the cabinet to become Secretary-General of the LDP.[^cite_note-165] However, on 20 October two of the women promoted in the reshuffle, Justice Minister [Midori Matsushima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midori_Matsushima) and Trade Minister [Yūko Obuchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C5%ABko_Obuchi), were forced to resign in separate election finance scandals. Abe told the press, "As prime minister, I bear full responsibility for having appointed them, and I apologize deeply to the people of Japan."[^cite_note-166]

#### 2014 general election

See also: [2014 Japanese general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Japanese_general_election)

Abe giving a speech in front of the Gundam Cafe in [Akihabara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akihabara), 2014

In November 2014, while Abe was attending the APEC forum meeting in China and the [G20 Summit in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_G20_Brisbane_summit), rumors began appearing in the press that he was planning to call a snap election in the event that he decided to delay the second stage of the consumption tax increase.[^cite_note-167] It was speculated that Abe planned to do this to "reset" Diet business after it had become gridlocked due to the fallout from ministerial resignations in October, or because the political situation would be less favorable to re-election in 2015 and 2016.[^cite_note-168]

On 17 November, GDP figures were released that showed Japan had fallen into recession as per the two-quarters of negative growth following the first stage of the consumption tax rise in April.[^cite_note-BBC20141117-100] Abe held a press conference on 21 November and announced that he was delaying the rise in the consumption tax by 18 months, from October 2015 to April 2017, and calling a snap general election for 14 December. Abe described the election as the "Abenomics Dissolution" and asked the voters to pass judgment on his economic policies.[^cite_note-169] The opposition parties attempted to field a united front in opposition to Abe's policies, but found themselves divided on them.[^cite_note-170]

In the [2014 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Japanese_general_election), the LDP won 291 seats, a loss of 3, but the [Komeito](163859.md) gained 4 to win 35. Therefore, the governing coalition maintained its two-thirds majority in a slightly reduced lower house of 475.[^cite_note-171]

### Third Cabinet (2014–2017)

Main article: [Third Abe cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Abe_cabinet)



Abe Cabinet approval ratings from 2012 to 2017

On 24 December 2014, Abe was re-elected to the position of prime minister by the House of Representatives. The only change he made when introducing his [third cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Abe_cabinet) was replacing defense minister [Akinori Eto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akinori_Eto), who was also involved in a political funding controversy, with [Gen Nakatani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gen_Nakatani).[^cite_note-172] In his February policy speech, as the Cabinet weathered a [Moritomo Gakuen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moritomo_Gakuen) school scandal, Abe called upon the new Diet to enact "most drastic reforms since the end of World War II" in the sectors of the economy, agriculture, healthcare and others.[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174]

#### Foreign policy



Abe addressing the [United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/114th_United_States_Congress), 2015

On a tour of the Middle East in January 2015, Abe announced that Japan would provide 200 million dollars in non-military assistance to countries fighting against the [Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant](9087364.md) as part of a 2.5-billion-dollar aid package.[^cite_note-175] Shortly after this, ISIL released a video in which a masked figure (identified as Mohammed Emwazi or "[Jihadi John](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadi_John)") threatened to kill two Japanese hostages, [Kenji Goto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenji_Goto) and Haruna Yukawa, in retaliation for the move unless Abe's government paid 200 million dollars of ransom money. Abe cut short his trip to deal with the crisis, declared that such acts of [terrorism](30636.md) were "unforgivable" and promised to save the hostages while refusing to pay the ransom.[^cite_note-176] The Abe cabinet worked with the Jordanian government to attempt to secure the release of both hostages, after further videos were released by ISIL linking their fate to that of the pilot [Muath al-Kasasbeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muath_al-Kasasbeh), with deputy foreign minister [Yasuhide Nakayama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasuhide_Nakayama) conducting negotiations in [Amman](45007.md).[^cite_note-177] Both hostages were killed with ISIL releasing news of Yukawa's death on 24 January and Goto's on 31 January. Abe condemned the killings as a "heinous act", declared that Japan would "not give in to terrorism" and pledged to work with the international community to bring the killers to justice.[^cite_note-178] There was some criticism of Abe for his move to pledge aid against ISIL while they were holding Japanese citizens hostage, but polls showed support for his administration increasing in the aftermath of the crisis.[^cite_note-179] He later used the example of the hostage crisis to argue the case for the collective self-defense legislation that his government introduced in the summer of 2015 (see below).[^cite_note-180]

In April 2015, Abe addressed a [joint session of the United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_the_United_States_Congress), the first Japanese prime minister to do so. In his speech he referred to the Japan–US Alliance as the "Alliance of Hope", promised that Japan would play a more active security and defense role in the alliance and argued that the TPP would bring both economic and security benefits to the Asia-Pacific region.[^cite_note-mofa.go.jp-181][^cite_note-182] The address served as part of a state visit to the United States, the eighth of the Obama Presidency, which President Obama referred to as a "celebration of the ties of friendship" between America and Japan. During the visit, Abe attended a state dinner at the White House.[^cite_note-183]

Like his predecessors [Tomiichi Murayama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomiichi_Murayama) and Junichiro Koizumi, Abe issued a statement commemorating the 70th anniversary of the [end of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_World_War_II_in_Asia) on 14 August 2015. This statement had been widely anticipated, with some commentators expecting Abe to amend or even refuse to repeat the previous leaders' apologies for Japan's role in the war.[^cite_note-184] In the statement, Abe committed to upholding the previous apologies and expressed "profound grief and eternal, sincere condolences" for the "immeasurable damage and suffering" Japan had caused for "innocent people" during the conflict. He also argued that Japan should not be "predestined to apologize" forever, noting that more than eighty percent of Japanese people alive today were born after the conflict and played no part in it.[^cite_note-185][^cite_note-186] The governments of both China and South Korea responded with criticism of the statement, but analysts noted that it was muted and restrained in tone, in comparison to the harsher rhetoric than had been employed previously.[^cite_note-187] A representative of the [US National Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_National_Security_Council) welcomed the statement and referred to Japan as having been a "model for nations everywhere" in its record on "peace, democracy, and the rule of law" since the war's end.[^cite_note-188] Professor [Gerald Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerald_Curtis) of [Columbia University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_University) argued that the statement "probably satisfies no constituency" either in Japan or abroad, but that by repeating the words "aggression", "colonialism", "apology" and "remorse" used in the [Murayama Statement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murayama_Statement) of 1995, it was likely to be enough to improve relations with China and Korea.[^cite_note-189]



Abe with Indian prime minister [Narendra Modi](444222.md) at the [Regional Comprehensive Economic Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_Comprehensive_Economic_Partnership) summit, 2017

In Seoul in November 2015, Abe attended the first [China–Japan–South Korea trilateral summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_trilateral_summit) held for three years with Korean president Park Geun-hye and Chinese premier [Li Keqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Keqiang). The summits had been suspended in 2012 due to tensions over historical and territorial issues. The leaders agreed to restore the summits as annual events, negotiate a trilateral free trade agreement, and work to check [North Korea's nuclear weapons programme](106424.md). They also announced that trilateral co-operation had been "completely restored".[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192] In December 2015, Abe and Indian prime minister Narendra Modi signed deals in which India agreed to buy *[Shinkansen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinkansen)* technology from Japan (financed in part by a loan from the Japanese government), and for Japan to be raised to full partner status in the [Malabar naval exercises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exercise_Malabar). Also agreed at the talks was a proposal for Japan to sell non-military nuclear technology to India, to be formally signed once technical details were finalized.[^cite_note-193]

[Japan's relations with South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan's_relations_with_South_Korea) improved somewhat during Abe's third term, in the aftermath of Abe's war anniversary statement.[^cite_note-194] Abe and South Korea's president Park Geun-hye held their first bilateral meeting in November 2015, where they agreed to resolve the "[comfort women](302767.md)" dispute, which Park described as the biggest obstacle to closer ties.[^cite_note-The_Guardian-147] In late December 2015, foreign ministers Fumio Kishida and [Yun Byung-se](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yun_Byung-se) announced in Seoul that a deal had been reached to resolve the comfort women dispute, in which Japan agreed to pay 1 billion yen (US\$8.3 million) into a fund to support the 46 surviving victims, and issued a statement that contained Abe's "most sincere apologies and remorse".[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-New_Yorker-196] Abe later telephoned Park. In return, the South Korean government agreed to consider the matter "finally and irreversibly resolved" and work to remove a statue in front of the Japanese embassy in Seoul. Both sides agreed to refrain from criticizing each other over the issue in the future. President Park stated that the agreement would be a "new starting point" for relations between the two countries, although both leaders received some domestic criticism: Abe for issuing the apology, and Park for accepting the deal.[^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]&#x20;



Abe with Obama at the [Hiroshima Peace Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiroshima_Peace_Memorial), 2016

On 27 May 2016, Abe accompanied Barack Obama when he became the first sitting US president to visit [Hiroshima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiroshima), 71 years after the [US atomic bombing of the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_bombings_of_Hiroshima_and_Nagasaki) towards the end of World War II. The two paid tribute to the victims of the bombing at the [Hiroshima Peace Memorial Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiroshima_Peace_Memorial_Museum), during the visit both leaders pledged to promote [nuclear disarmament](22165.md).[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] On 27 December 2016, Abe paid a reciprocal visit to [USS *Arizona* Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Arizona_Memorial), Honolulu.[^cite_note-202] The Abe government and the Japanese public mistakenly thought Abe's visit of Honolulu was unprecedented. Abe's visit drew public attention for the first time to the three quiet visits to Honolulu by Japanese prime ministers in 1951, 1956, and 1957.[^cite_note-203]



Abe and US president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in 2017 with hats reading "Donald & Shinzo, Make Alliance Even Greater"

Shortly after Donald Trump had won the [US presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election), Abe cut short his presence at an Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit being held in [Lima, Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lima,_Peru), in order to have an informal, impromptu meeting with the then president-elect at the [Trump Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Tower).[^cite_note-204] After Trump's inauguration, they had a formal meeting at [Mar-a-Lago](1452754.md) in [Palm Beach, Florida](109617.md), at which they discussed security in light of a North Korean threat,[^cite_note-205] with Abe stating that Japan would be more committed to [Japan–United States relations](2991012.md).[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207]

#### Security and defense issues

In his April speech to the US Congress, Abe announced that his government would "enact all necessary bills by this coming summer" to expand the Self-Defense Forces' capacity for operations and to give effect to the cabinet's July 2014 decision to reinterpret the constitution in favor of collective self-defense.[^cite_note-mofa.go.jp-181] The Abe cabinet then introduced 11 bills making up the [Legislation for Peace and Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislation_for_Peace_and_Security) into the Diet in May 2015, which pushed for a limited expansion of military powers to fight in a foreign conflict. The principal aims of the bills were to allow Japan's Self-Defense Forces to come to the aid of allied nations under attack (even if Japan itself was not) in an "existential crisis situation", to expand their scope to support international peacekeeping operations, and to allow for Japan to take on a greater share of security responsibilities as part of the [US-Japan Alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US-Japan_Alliance).[^cite_note-reference_to_NY_times-208][^cite_note-The_Diplomat-209][^cite_note-210]



Abe with US secretary of defense [Jim Mattis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Mattis) in 2017

To allow for enough time to pass the bills in the face of lengthy opposition scrutiny, the Abe cabinet extended the Diet session by 95 days from June into September, making it the longest in the post-war era.[^cite_note-211] The bills passed the House of Representatives on 16 July with the support of the majority LDP-Komeito coalition. Diet members from the opposition parties walked out of the vote in protest at what they said was the government's move to force the bills through without sufficient debate.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213] Abe countered by arguing that the bills had been debated for "as many as 113 hours" before the vote.[^cite_note-214] A government using its majority to "railroad" controversial bills through the Diet in the face of political and public opposition is a subject of criticism in Japan.[^cite_note-215] As a result of these moves, Abe faced a public backlash, and opinion polls showed that his approval ratings fell into negative figures for the first time since he returned to power in 2012.[^cite_note-216] Many protested outside the Diet buildings, denouncing what was referred to as "war bills" by opponents.[^cite_note-reference_to_NY_times-208] The security bills were finally approved by the House of Councillors, 148 votes to 90, and became law on 19 September.[^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

In December 2015, the Abe government announced the creation of a new intelligence unit, the [International Counterterrorism Intelligence Collection Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Counterterrorism_Intelligence_Collection_Unit?action=edit\&redlink=1), to aid counter-terrorism operations, to be based in the Foreign Ministry but led by the prime minister's office. This was reported as being part of efforts to step up security measures in preparation for the [2016 G7 Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_G7_Summit) in [Shima, Mie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shima,_Mie), and [2020 Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Olympics) in Tokyo.[^cite_note-219] In the same month the cabinet approved Japan's largest-ever defense budget, at 5.1 trillion yen (US\$45 billion), for the fiscal year beginning in April 2016. The package included funding intended for the purchase of three ["Global Hawk" drones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northrop_Grumman_RQ-4_Global_Hawk), six [F-35 fighter jets](11812.md), and a [Boeing KC-46A midair refueling aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_KC-46_Pegasus).[^cite_note-220]

#### Re-election as LDP president and "Abenomics 2.0"

See also: [2015 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election)



Abe speaking at the [Hudson Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hudson_Institute) in Washington, D.C., 2016

In September 2015, Abe was re-elected as president of the LDP in an uncontested election.[^cite_note-221] Following this Abe carried out a cabinet reshuffle, once again keeping the key ministers of [Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_of_Finance_\(Japan\)), Economy, Foreign Affairs, and the Chief Cabinet Secretary in post. He also created a new ministerial position for the coordination of policies related to the economy, population decline, and social security reform, which was filled by [Katsunobu Katō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsunobu_Kat%C5%8D).[^cite_note-222]

At a press conference after his official re-election as LDP president, Abe announced that the next stage of his administration would focus on what he called "Abenomics 2.0", the aim of which was to tackle issues of [low fertility and an aging population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demography_of_Japan) and create a society "in which each and every one of Japan's 100 million citizens can take on active roles".[^cite_note-ReferenceA-223] This new policy consisted of targets which Abe referred to as "three new arrows"; to boost Japan's GDP to 600 trillion yen by 2021, to raise the national fertility rate from an average of 1.4 to 1.8 children per woman and stabilize the population at 100 million, and to create a situation where people would not have to leave employment to care for elderly relatives by the mid-2020s. Abe explained that the government would take measures to increase wages, boost consumption, and expand childcare, social security and care services for the elderly to meet these goals.[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-225]

This new iteration of Abenomics was met with some criticism by commentators, who argued that it was not yet clear if the first three arrows had succeeded in lifting Japan out of deflation (inflation was some way below the 2 percent target), that the new arrows were merely presented as targets without the necessary policies to meet them, and that the targets themselves were unrealistic.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228] However, opinion polls during the final months of 2015 showed the Abe cabinet's approval ratings once again climbing into positive figures after the change in emphasis back to economic issues.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230]
