Abe led the conclusion of the Trans-Pacific Partnership talks in early October 2015.[^cite_note-231] In December 2015, the two parties making up Abe's governing coalition agreed to introduce a reduced rate of consumption tax for food when the anticipated tax increase from 8 to 10 percent takes place in April 2017.[^cite_note-232][^cite_note-233][^cite_note-234] Abe dismissed the chairman of the LDP's tax panel [Takeshi Noda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takeshi_Noda) (who opposed the reduction), and appointed [Yoichi Miyazawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoichi_Miyazawa), who was more favorable to the policy, as his replacement.[^cite_note-235]

#### Constitutional revision

Main article: [Proposed Japanese constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_Japanese_constitutional_referendum)

At the 2016 election to the House of Councillors, the first that allowed Japanese citizens 18 and over to vote, Abe led the LDP–Komeito pact to victory, with the coalition being the largest in the House of Councillors since it was set at 242 seats. The election's results opened the debate on constitutional reform, particularly in amending Article 9 of Japan's pacifist constitution, with pro-revisionist parties gaining the two-thirds majority being necessary for reform, alongside a two-thirds majority in the House of Representatives, which would ultimately lead to a nationwide referendum.[^cite_note-236] Abe remained relatively quiet on the issue for the remainder of the year, but in May 2017, announced that the constitutional reform would be in effect by 2020.[^cite_note-237]

### Fourth Cabinet (2017–2020)

Main article: [Fourth Abe cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Abe_cabinet)

The [2017 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Japanese_general_election) was held on 22 October. Prime Minister Abe called the snap election on 25 September, while the [North Korea crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932018_North_Korea_crisis) was prominent in the news media.[^cite_note-veconomist-238] Abe was expected to retain a majority of seats in the Diet.[^cite_note-239] Abe's ruling coalition took almost a majority of the vote and two-thirds of the seats.

#### Re-election as LDP president

See also: [2018 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election)

On 20 September 2018, Abe was re-elected as leader of the main ruling Liberal Democratic Party.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] On 19 November 2019, Abe became Japan's longest-serving prime minister, surpassing the 2,883-day record of [Katsura Tarō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katsura_Tar%C5%8D).[^cite_note-ft-242] On 24 August 2020, Abe became the longest-serving prime minister in terms of consecutive days in office, surpassing Eisaku Satō's 2,798-day record.[^cite_note-243]

#### Favoritism scandals

In March 2018, it was revealed that the finance ministry (with finance minister Tarō Asō at its head) had falsified documents presented to the parliament in relation to the [Moritomo Gakuen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moritomo_Gakuen) scandal, in order to remove 14 passages implicating Abe.[^cite_note-financialtimes-244] Further accusations arose the same year that Abe had given preferential treatment to his friend Kotarō Kake to open a veterinary department at his school, Kake Gakuen. Abe denied the charges, but support for his administration fell below 30% in the polls, the lowest since his taking power in 2012. The scandal was referred to by some as "Abegate".[^cite_note-245]

The scandals, while not damaging his political standing permanently, did little good for his image. In July 2018, Abe's public standing was further hit after he held a drinking party with LDP lawmakers during the peak of the [floods in western Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Japan_floods).[^cite_note-246] In 2020, Abe came under further criticism for extending the term of top Tokyo prosecutor [Hiromu Kurokawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiromu_Kurokawa), who later resigned amid a gambling scandal. Abe's approval rating fell from 40% to 27% during the month of May 2020, largely due to his handling of the Kurokawa situation.[^cite_note-247]

#### Foreign policy



Abe with Senegalese President [Macky Sall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macky_Sall) in 2017.

Abe supported the [2018 North Korea–United States summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_North_Korea%E2%80%93United_States_Singapore_Summit). Abe, however, cautioned President Trump not to compromise on North Korea's missile program and leave Japan exposed to [short-range missiles](1320069.md) or relieve pressure on North Korea too soon before complete denuclearization.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] Reports in 2019 revealed that Abe authorized covering up information about two missing people from Japan living in North Korea.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251]



Abe with CCP General Secretary and Chinese President [Xi Jinping](2017814.md) in November 2017.

In 2018, Abe paid a formal visit to China, in the hopes of improving foreign relations, where he had several meetings with Chinese leader Xi Jinping. Abe stated that he hoped Xi Jinping would visit Japan to cultivate better relations between the two countries.[^cite_note-252] At the [G20 Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_G20_Osaka_summit). Abe told Xi it is important for "a free and open Hong Kong to prosper under the 'one country, two systems' policy".[^cite_note-253]

Regarding the territorial dispute with Russia,[^cite_note-254] Abe adopted the "two plus alpha" approach, altering the previous governments' position that Habomai, Shikotan, Etorofu and Kunashiri islands be returned to Japan's sovereign.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] In a 2021 interview, Abe spoke about his negotiation with the Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in 2018, also confirming he had requested Fumio Kishida to continue with this approach, to avoid worsening Japan's relations with Russia.[^cite_note-257]



Abe with Canadian Prime Minister [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau) in June 2018



Abe with French President [Emmanuel Macron](43671127.md) in October 2018



Abe with Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) in January 2019

Japan's relations with South Korea further deteriorated starting from 2018,[^cite_note-Reuters2019-258][^cite_note-Ju2020-259] when, while negotiating a "comfort women" deal with then South Korean president [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in), Abe demanded that South Korea remove statues of comfort women that had been installed in South Korea, the United States, Australia, and Germany.[^cite_note-260] In late 2018, the [Supreme Court of Korea](4899540.md) and other high courts ordered several Japanese companies to make compensations to the families of Koreans who were unfairly treated and illegally forced to supply labor for World War II war efforts. The Japanese government protested these decisions, Abe argued that any issues concerning Japan's rule of Korea were previously resolved in the [Treaty on Basic Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_Basic_Relations_Between_Japan_and_the_Republic_of_Korea) which normalized relations between Japan and South Korea, adding that further requests of reparations meant that South Korea had violated the treaty. In August 2019, Abe's cabinet approved the removal of South Korea from Japan's trade "whitelist"; the subsequent [trade dispute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_trade_dispute) between South Korea and Japan causeing a significant deterioration in [Japan–South Korea relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_relations).[^cite_note-Reuters2019-258][^cite_note-Ju2020-259]

#### Economic policy

In July 2018, Japan became the second country after Mexico to ratify the [Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comprehensive_and_Progressive_Agreement_for_Trans-Pacific_Partnership) (CPTPP). CPTPP evolved from the Trans-Pacific Partnership which never came into force after then US president Donald Trump withdrew the United States from the agreement in early 2017. Abe's administration was credited with overcoming protectionist pressures within Japan and rallying the 10 other TPP member countries to support CPTPP, which largely kept the previous agreement intact and left the door open to an eventual US return.[^cite_note-Bremmer2022-261][^cite_note-Rogin2022-262][^cite_note-Goodman2022-263]

#### Resignation

See also: [2020 Liberal Democratic Party presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Liberal_Democratic_Party_presidential_election)



Abe bowing after announcing his resignation during a press conference in Tokyo, August 2020

Abe's colitis relapsed in June 2020 and resulted in his health deteriorating through the summer. Following several hospital visits, Abe announced on 28 August 2020 that he intended to retire as prime minister, citing his inability to carry out the duties of the office while seeking treatment for his condition.[^cite_note-264] During the press conference announcing his retirement, Abe indicated that he would remain in office until [a successor was chosen by the LDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Liberal_Democratic_Party_\(Japan\)_leadership_election), but declined to endorse any specific successor.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266] Abe expressed regret at being unable to fully accomplish his policy goals due to his early retirement.[^cite_note-267] [Chief cabinet secretary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_cabinet_secretary) Yoshihide Suga was elected as his successor by the LDP on 14 September 2020 and took office as prime minister on 16 September.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] In retirement, Abe was not as active as before. He continued to give interviews sporadically, such as the career retrospective on 19 July 2021, he gave to [H.R. McMaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H.R._McMaster) of the [Hoover Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoover_Institute).[^cite_note-mcmaster21-270]

## Assassination

Main article: [Assassination of Shinzo Abe](71250084.md)

Exterior of Yamato-Saidaiji Station

The site of Abe's [assassination](71250084.md) on 8 July 2022, just hours after the shooting

On 8 July 2022, at around 11\:30 JST,[^cite_note-271][^cite_note-272] Abe was shot and mortally wounded while he was delivering a campaign speech at the [Yamato-Saidaiji Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamato-Saidaiji_Station), [Nara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nara,_Nara).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-abedeath-274][^cite_note-Death-2] He was supporting fellow LDP party member [Kei Satō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kei_Sat%C5%8D_\(politician\)) for the [House of Councillors election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Japanese_House_of_Councillors_election).[^cite_note-abedeath_eng-275] The assassin approached Abe from behind and fired two shots using an [improvised firearm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improvised_firearm). The first shot missed and prompted Abe to turn around, at which point a second shot was fired, hitting Abe in the right front of the neck and the left upper arm. He was fatally struck in an artery near the heart.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278][^cite_note-FNN2022-279]

Abe was rushed to the [Nara Medical University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nara_Medical_University_Hospital) in [Kashihara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kashihara) by helicopter. Prior to his arrival at the hospital, Abe had already shown no [vital signs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vital_signs). Despite a [transfusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_transfusion) of 100 units of blood (≈ 30 liters),[^cite_note-280] the [blood loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_loss) was too great and attempts to resuscitate Abe failed;[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] he died there aged 67 at 17\:03 JST.[^cite_note-abedeath_eng-275][^cite_note-283][^cite_note-Death-2]

A 41-year-old man named [Tetsuya Yamagami](71250226.md), a former [JMSDF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JMSDF) member, was immediately arrested and later confessed to local police.[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285] Yamagami said he held a grudge against the [Unification Church](512933.md)[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] and shot Abe because "the religious group and Abe were connected".[^cite_note-FNN2022-279][^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] Yamagami said his mother had been brainwashed by the religious group, giving the church all of his family's money[^cite_note-mainichi090722-291][^cite_note-yomiuri090722-292] and leaving them so destitute that he and his siblings often did not have enough to eat. They became so despondent, he added, that his brother had committed suicide and he himself had attempted it.[^cite_note-Worth-Moonies-Atlantic-293]

Yamagami had been trying to kill [Hak Ja Han](577802.md) of the Unification Church since around 2002, but gave up because he could not get close to her, changing his target to Abe.[^cite_note-294] Yamagami told investigators that he did not harbor any resentment for Abe's political beliefs, but had targeted him because he believed the former prime minister was responsible for spreading the religion in Japan.[^cite_note-mainichi090722-291][^cite_note-yomiuri090722-292] Abe and his family were known to have long-standing ties to the Unification Church, dating back to his grandfather Nobusuke Kishi; Abe himself had held speeches in support of the religious movement.[^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296] According to research by *[Nikkan Gendai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikkan_Gendai)*, half of the twenty members of the Fourth Abe Cabinet had connections to the Unification Church.[^cite_note-297] The connection between the two groups was found peculiar by some as the South Korean-based Unification Church raised billions of dollars' worth of funds from its Japanese followers ("since the 1970s" most of the church's money has come from Japan),[^cite_note-298] playing on feelings of "war guilt" towards Korea, while Abe was famous for his proudly unapologetic embrace of Japan's imperial past and denial of any oppression of Koreans.[^cite_note-Worth-Moonies-Atlantic-293]

In response to the shooting and his subsequent death, numerous present and former world leaders expressed their sympathies and support for Abe.[^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] His body was returned to Tokyo the day after his assassination and his state funeral took place on 27 September 2022.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] Yamagami was tried and convicted of Abe's murder, and sentenced to life imprisonment on 21 January 2026.[^cite_note-303]

## Political positions

&#x20;Some have described Abe as a right-wing [Japanese nationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_nationalist).[^cite_note-Justin_McCurry-304] In a 1996 book he co-wrote with two other lawmakers, Abe credits his grandfather Nobusuke Kishi as the source of his conservatism. In his memoir *Towards a Beautiful Country*, he writes that "A strong state is not a potential threat to political liberty that must be restrained by constitutionalism and civil society. It is the ultimate guarantor of national independence in a dangerous world governed by the law of the jungle". While accepting that strong states might be used against its citizens, he rejects the view that oppression is inherent in strong states but is instead caused by "closed governments" dominated by a small number of people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris202047-24]

### Historical negationism

Some have claimed that Abe often engaged in [historical negationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_negationism) via his membership in [Nippon Kaigi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippon_Kaigi), especially in regard to [Japanese war crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_war_crimes) during World War II.[^cite_note-NaruMun-305][^cite_note-Rupert_Wingfield-Hayes-306] According to some analysts, this caused Japan's relations with South Korea and China to deteriorate under his premiership.[^cite_note-foreignpolicy_2020-09-307] In China, Abe's legacy remains mixed. While he was often criticized as an extreme nationalist, others also recognized his efforts for reconciliation between the two countries.[^cite_note-308] Since 1997, as the bureau chief of the "Institute of Junior Assembly Members Who Think About the Outlook of Japan and History Education", Abe led the [Society for History Textbook Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_for_History_Textbook_Reform). Abe was affiliated with the ultra-conservative, revisionist organization Nippon Kaigi (Japan Conference).[^cite_note-ABCMC-309][^cite_note-Tea_Party_Politics_in_Japan-310]

According to Alexis Dudden, a professor of history at the [University of Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Connecticut) who specializes in modern Japan and Korea, in the 1990s when Abe first became a parliamentarian he is believed to have co-authored a document denying the [Nanjing Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing_Massacre); the article used to be available in Japan's Diet archives but has since disappeared. Abe also made comments that denied the occurrence of the massacre. Dudden has said [Nanjing Massacre denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanjing_Massacre_denial) is akin to [Holocaust denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial).[^cite_note-New_Yorker-196]

On 1 March 2007, Abe denied to reporters that Japan forced women into sexual slavery during World War II, claiming that there was "no evidence" for that.[^cite_note-Onishi2007-51][^cite_note-\:0-311] He elaborated that he meant "forcible in the narrow sense of the word". On 26 March 2007, he apologized, saying: "I express my sympathy toward the comfort women and apologize for the situation they found themselves in".[^cite_note-\:0-311] On his official homepage,[^cite_note-312] he questioned the extent to which coercion was applied toward the comfort women, dismissing South Korean positions on the issue as foreign interference in Japanese domestic affairs; Abe's position towards the comfort women caused deteriorations of relations between Japan and South Korea.[^cite_note-foreignpolicy_2020-09-307][^cite_note-313] In a Diet session on 6 October 2006, Abe revised his statement regarding comfort women and said that he accepted the report issued in 1993 by the sitting cabinet secretary, [Yōhei Kōno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Y%C5%8Dhei_K%C5%8Dno), wherein the Japanese government officially acknowledged the issue. Later in the session, Abe stated his belief that Class-A [war criminals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_criminals) are not criminals under Japan's domestic law.[^cite_note-314]

In a meeting of the Lower House Budget Committee in February 2006, Abe said, "There is a problem as to how to define aggressive wars; we cannot say it is decided academically",[^cite_note-20060216minutes-315] and "It is not the business of the government to decide how to define the last world war. I think we have to wait for the estimation of historians".[^cite_note-20060216minutes-315]



Abe with [Park Geun-hye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Geun-hye) and [Li Keqiang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Keqiang) at the 2015 [China–Japan–South Korea trilateral summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_trilateral_summit)

Abe published a book called *Toward a Beautiful Nation* (美しい国へ, *Utsukushii kuni e*) in July 2006, which became a bestseller in Japan. The South Korean and Chinese governments, as well as noted academics and commentators, voiced concern about Abe's historical views.[^cite_note-316][^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

In March 2007, in response to a [United States Congress](31756.md) resolution introduced by [Mike Honda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Honda), Abe denied any government coercion in the recruitment of comfort women during World War II.[^cite_note-319] This was in line with a statement made almost 10 years earlier, in which Abe voiced his opposition to the inclusion of the subject of military prostitution in several school textbooks while denying any coercion in the "narrow" sense of the word, environmental factors notwithstanding.[^cite_note-japan-press.co.jp-320] This statement provoked negative reactions in Asian and [western](73162.md) countries; a *[New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Times)* editorial on 6 March 2007 commented for instance:

> &#x20;What part of 'Japanese Army sex slaves' does Japan's prime minister, Shinzo Abe, have so much trouble understanding and apologizing for?… These were not commercial brothels. Force, explicit and implicit, was used in recruiting these women. What went on in them was serial rape, not prostitution. The Japanese Army's involvement is documented in the government's own defense files. A senior Tokyo official more or less apologized for this horrific crime in 1993… Yesterday, \[Abe] grudgingly acknowledged the 1993 quasi-apology, but only as part of a pre-emptive declaration that his government would reject the call, now pending in the United States Congress, for an official apology. America isn't the only country interested in seeing Japan belatedly accept full responsibility. \[South] Korea and China are also infuriated by years of Japanese equivocations over the issue.[^cite_note-321]

A 2007 *[Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Post)* editorial, "Shinzo Abe's Double Talk", also criticized him: "he's passionate about Japanese victims of North Korea—and blind to Japan's own war crimes".[^cite_note-322] In *The New York Times* in 2014, an editorial called Abe a "nationalist" who was a profound threat to American–Japanese relations,[^cite_note-323] and an opinion piece labeled Abe's position on the subject of comfort women a "war on truth".[^cite_note-324] The same editorial presented him as a revisionist, a view largely accepted by the international and part of the Japanese press.[^cite_note-325][^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328] Writing in the *[London Review of Books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Review_of_Books)*, political scientist [Edward Luttwak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Luttwak) called Abe a "pragmatic Japanese Tory driving through reforms at home, while weaving an alliance aimed at containing China".[^cite_note-329]



Abe meeting with South Korean president [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in) in 2018

### Response to the mass media

The *[Asahi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asahi_Shimbun)* accused Abe and [Shōichi Nakagawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sh%C5%8Dichi_Nakagawa) of censoring a 2001 [NHK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NHK) program concerning "The Women's International War Crimes Tribunal".[^cite_note-330] The "tribunal" was a private committee which was established to adjudicate the complaints of [comfort women](302767.md); about 5,000 people, including 64 victims from Japan and abroad, attended. The members of the committee, who claimed to be specialists in [international law](8195726.md), claimed that Emperor [Hirohito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hirohito) and the Japanese government were responsible for the use of comfort women. However, the TV program did not mention the full name of the tribunal, it omitted keywords such as "Japanese troops" and "[sexual slavery](173634.md)", and it also cut the sight of the tribunal, the host grouping, the statements of the organizer, and the judgment itself. Instead, it presented criticism of the tribunal by a right-wing academic who stated that "there was no abduction of sex slaves and they were prostitutes".[^cite_note-331]

On the day following the broadcasting of the report by *Asahi Shimbun*, Akira Nagai, the chief producer and the primary person who was responsible for the program, held a press conference in which he stated that Abe stated that the content of the report by the *Asahi Shimbun*. "had to be broadcast from a neutral point of view" and "what I did is not giving into political pressure". Abe said, "It was political terrorism by *Asahi Shimbun* and it was tremendously clear that they had the intention to inhume me and Mr. Nakagawa politically, and it is also clear that it was a complete fabrication." He also characterized the tribunal as a "mock trial" and raised objection to the presence of North Korean [prosecutors](440936.md), singling them out as agents of the North Korean government.[^cite_note-332] Abe's actions in the NHK incident were criticized by journalists as violating both the Broadcasting Act and the constitution.[^cite_note-333]

On 24 October 2006, a report which stated that Abe's new administration had called on the NHK to "pay attention" to the North Korean abductees issue emerged.[^cite_note-IHT06-334] Critics, some even within Abe's own LDP party, charged that the government was violating freedom of expression by meddling in the affairs of the public broadcaster.[^cite_note-IHT06-334] In December 2006, it was revealed that former prime minister Junichiro Koizumi's government, in which Abe was Chief Cabinet Secretary, had influenced town hall-style meetings, during which paid performers would ask government officials favorable questions.[^cite_note-335]

On 22 November 2012, it was reported that [TBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TBS_Television_\(Japan\))'s early morning TV show *Asazuba* accidentally displayed Abe's photo alongside a news report about an NHK announcer's arrest for a sex offense. Abe's face filled viewers' screens along with the name of NHK announcer Takeshige Morimoto, who anchors NHK's *Ohayo Nippon* program on Saturday and Sunday. Morimoto was arrested for allegedly groping a woman on the train. Abe posted on his public [Facebook page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook_page), "This morning on the TBS show *Asazuba*, when a newscaster reported on a story regarding the apprehension of a molester, a photo of me was shown. Images of this blunder can now be seen clearly across the Internet, Have the slander campaigns already begun!? If this were merely an accident, it would be proper for the TV station to give me a personal apology, but as yet I haven't heard a single word." The newscaster acknowledged that the incorrect image had been displayed, but merely stated that the photo was "unrelated" and did not refer to the politician by name. Neither Abe nor his office received any form of apology.[^cite_note-336]

Abe's constant interference and intimidation of media outlets was cited as one of many reasons Japan fell to 72nd place on the [Press Freedom Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_Freedom_Index) in 2016, in contrast to its previous 11th-place ranking from six years prior.[^cite_note-337]

See also: [Controversies surrounding Yasukuni Shrine § Shinzo Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controversies_surrounding_Yasukuni_Shrine#Shinzo_Abe)

Abe visited the Yasukuni Shrine on several occasions, which enshrines the spirits of Japan's war dead, including several Class-A war criminals convicted in the [International Military Tribunal for the Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Military_Tribunal_for_the_Far_East). While serving as Chief Cabinet Secretary in the government of Junichiro Koizumi, he visited in April 2006, prompting South Korea to describe the trip as "regrettable".[^cite_note-338] He visited again on 15 August 2012, the anniversary of the end of World War II.[^cite_note-339] After winning the presidency of the Liberal Democratic Party, he visited on 17 October 2012, in an official capacity as party president.[^cite_note-340]

Abe initially refrained from visiting the shrine as a sitting prime minister. He did not visit at all during his first term from September 2006 to September 2007, unlike his predecessor Koizumi, who had visited yearly while in office. Abe's not visiting the shrine prompted a Japanese nationalist named Yoshihiro Tanjo to cut off his little finger in protest and mail it to the LDP.[^cite_note-341] While campaigning for the presidency of the LDP in 2012, Abe said that he regretted not visiting the shrine while he was prime minister. He again refrained from visiting the shrine during the first year of his second stint as prime minister in consideration of improving relations with China and Korea, whose leaders refused to meet with Abe during this time. He said on 9 December 2013 that "it is natural that we should express our feelings of respect to the war dead who sacrificed their lives for the nation... but it is my thinking that we should avoid making \[Yasukuni visits] political and diplomatic issues". In lieu of visiting, Abe sent ritual offerings to the shrine for festivals in April and October 2013, as well as the anniversary of the end of World War II in August 2013.[^cite_note-342]

His first visit to the shrine as prime minister took place on 26 December 2013, the first anniversary of his second term in office. It was the first visit to the shrine by a sitting prime minister since Junichiro Koizumi visited in August 2006. Abe said that he "prayed to pay respect for the war dead who sacrificed their precious lives and hoped that they rest in peace". The Chinese government published a protest that day, calling government visits to the shrine "an effort to glorify the Japanese militaristic history of external invasion and colonial rule and to challenge the outcome of World War II".[^cite_note-343] [Qin Gang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qin_Gang) of the [Chinese Ministry of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_the_People's_Republic_of_China) said Abe is "unwelcome by Chinese people... Chinese leaders won't meet him any more".[^cite_note-344] The *[Mainichi Shimbun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainichi_Shimbun)* argued in an editorial that the visit could also "cast a dark shadow" on relations with the United States.[^cite_note-345] The US embassy in Tokyo released a statement saying that "the United States is disappointed that Japan's leadership has taken an action that will exacerbate tensions with Japan's neighbors".[^cite_note-346] *[The Wall Street Journal](173070.md)* reported that US officials urged Abe not to visit the shrine and pay homage to war criminals anymore.[^cite_note-347] On 15 August 2014, the 69th anniversary of the surrender of Japan in World War II, Abe chose to not visit the shrine, in what was perceived as a diplomatic gesture to China, South Korea, and [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93Taiwan_relations). Despite Abe's absence, China and South Korea both voiced their disapproval at Japan's leadership as a large number of politicians, including three cabinet members, did attend the shrine to mark the anniversary.[^cite_note-AbeAbsence-348]

### Photographs with "731"



The Blue Impulse Kawasaki T-4 numbered 731 at Naha Airport in 2018

In May 2013, Abe posed for photographs giving [thumbs up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thumbs_up) gestures while sitting in the cockpit of a [Kawasaki T-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawasaki_T-4) military training aircraft of the [Japan Air Self-Defense Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan_Air_Self-Defense_Force)'s [Blue Impulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Impulse) [aerobatics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerobatics) team. The aircraft was numbered "731", which was the number of the infamous [Imperial Japanese Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Japanese_Army) [Unit 731](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_731) that conducted lethal chemical and biological experiments on live prisoners of war during World War II. The image was widely circulated in South Korean media and drew widespread criticism in both South Korea and China. The combination of the symbolically charged number and Abe's casual gesture was viewed as deeply insensitive and provocative.[^cite_note-349][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] South Korean politician [Chung Mong-joon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chung_Mong-joon) described Abe's actions as "an act of direct provocation to Korea, China and other victim nations". In response, a [Japanese Defense Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Defense_Ministry) official claimed that the numbering on the jet was coincidental and that the numbers were the pilot's individual ID number.[^cite_note-353][^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355]

### Restoration of Sovereignty Day

On 28 April 2013, a new public event, the Restoration of Sovereignty Day ([Japanese](15606.md): 主権回復の日, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Japanese): *Shuken kaifuku no hi*), was held in Tokyo to mark the 61st anniversary of the end of the [US occupation of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_occupation_of_Japan). It had been proposed by Abe in 2012. Since the [US occupation of Okinawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_occupation_of_Okinawa) ended in 1972 and nearly three-quarters of US troops in Japan continue to be stationed in Okinawa, the event, which was attended by Emperor [Akihito](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akihito), was denounced by many Okinawans, who saw it as celebrating a betrayal. There were demonstrations in both [Okinawa](22477.md) and Tokyo.[^cite_note-bbc_rsd-356]

### Immigration policy

In 2015, the Abe government refused to admit [refugees](45547.md) affected by conflicts in the Middle East and Africa. Abe said Japan must "solve its own problems before accepting immigrants." Abe backed a short-term work visa deal for migrant workers to "work for a limited period, earn more and return home."[^cite_note-357]

## Personal life

Abe disembarking from a plan with his wife, Akie Abe

Abe and his wife [Akie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akie_Abe) in 2016

Abe married [Akie Matsuzaki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akie_Abe), a [socialite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialite) and former radio disc jockey, in 1987. She is the daughter of the president of [Morinaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morinaga_&_Company), a [confectionery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confectionery) manufacturer. She is popularly known as the "domestic opposition party" due to her outspoken views, which often contradicted her husband's.[^cite_note-akie-358] Following her husband's first stint as prime minister, she opened an organic [izakaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Izakaya) in the [Kanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanda,_Tokyo) district of Tokyo, but remained inactive in management due to the urging of her mother-in-law.[^cite_note-akie-358] The couple were unable to have children, having undergone unsuccessful fertility treatments early in their marriage.[^cite_note-359]

Abe's elder brother, Hironobu Abe, became president and CEO of Mitsubishi Shōji Packaging Corporation, while his younger brother, [Nobuo Kishi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobuo_Kishi), became Senior Vice Minister for Foreign Affairs.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-361] Abe abstained from drinking alcohol.[^cite_note-362] In addition to his native Japanese, Abe spoke English.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364][^cite_note-365]

## Legacy



Abe's lifelong goal of revising the [Constitution of Japan](62699.md) and its [Article 9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_9_of_the_Japanese_Constitution) clause was unrealized at the time of his death.

Abe was often referred to as the "[shadow shogun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_shogun)" due to his profound influence on Japanese politics during his life.[^cite_note-wapolegacy-366] After his assassination, Japanologist [Michael Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Green_\(political_expert\)) described Abe as "the most consequential modern Japanese leader" and argued that Japan's future appears to be that of Abe's "vision".[^cite_note-367] Following Abe's assassination, the LDP–Komeito coalition won a majority of the available seats in the upper house in the 10 July election.[^cite_note-368] This gave the government a majority in the upper house.[^cite_note-369] Many, such as Sheila A. Smith of the [Council on Foreign Relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_on_Foreign_Relations) and East Asia expert [Jeff Kingston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Kingston), speculated that a revised constitution, especially a revision of its Article 9 clause which outlaws war and the maintenance of military forces, may become Abe's main legacy.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] Abe is also credited with engineering the Trans-Pacific Partnership (TPP), and salvaging the deal as the Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership (CPTPP), after the US withdrew from the TPP in 2017.[^cite_note-Bremmer2022-261][^cite_note-Rogin2022-262][^cite_note-Goodman2022-263]



Memorial monument for Shinzo Abe in Nara Japan (2023)



Memorial monument for Shinzo Abe at Osaka Gokoku Shrine in Osaka Japan (2025)

A *Washington Post* analysis described Abe as the "preeminent statesman of the [Indo-Pacific](330456.md)" whose realist approach helped build a cooperative security network in East Asia.[^cite_note-wapolegacy-366] Columnist [Josh Rogin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_Rogin) wrote that Abe's lasting legacy was a world better prepared to confront an increasingly assertive China.[^cite_note-Rogin2022-262] Similarly, US Admiral [James Stavridis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Stavridis) wrote that Abe's greatest contribution was a strengthened Japanese military.[^cite_note-372] His death solicited tributes and condolences from many states and leaders, a testament to his commitment to international relations.[^cite_note-373] Consultant [Bill Emmott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Emmott) noted that thanks to Abe's diplomacy, he was more popular abroad than domestically, bearing similarities to [Margaret Thatcher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher).[^cite_note-374] Economist Matthew P. Goodman of the [Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies) (CSIS) wrote that Abe's legacy was that of a "Champion of the Global Economic Order", whose administration launched the "Partnership for Quality Infrastructure" infrastructure project in Asia, proposed an internationally endorsed organizing principle for global [data governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_governance), kickstarted the TPP, and later salvaged it with the CPTPP.[^cite_note-Goodman2022-263] Indian commentator Harshil Mehta called Abe a "Unifier of Oceans" in his obituary due to formation of the Quad and wrote that he "stayed committed to the common cause" of Japan and India.[^cite_note-375] Conversely, Abe's nationalism and [historical denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_negationism#War_crimes) strained long-term relations with the neighboring China and South Korea.[^cite_note-New_Yorker-196] Abe's complex legacy was displayed in both nations following his assassination, where some praised Abe's efforts to improve relations and others denounced his views on Japanese history with its neighbors.[^cite_note-376][^cite_note-377] According to historian of religions Ernils Larsson, Abe's political rhetoric stoked "religious nationalism", and under his leadership the LDP forged closer ties with the "Shinto establishment".[^cite_note-378] In addition, journalist [Jake Adelstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jake_Adelstein) argued that Abe left a legacy vexed by authoritarianism, with his actions throughout his tenure such as the intimidation of media that were critical of him leading to Japan's fall on the Press Freedom Index to as low as 72nd, stoking [anti-Korean sentiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Korean_sentiment_in_Japan), as well as the creation of a "Cabinet Personnel Bureau" to vet bureaucratic positions of anyone that may be openly critical of the government, among others.[^cite_note-379]

In Japan, Abe remains controversial and is described as the "most polarizing" figure in contemporary Japanese politics.[^cite_note-nation-380][^cite_note-381] Domestic divisiveness created by his historical negationist efforts is long-lasting.[^cite_note-New_Yorker-196] Abe also had a profound and lasting effect on the Japanese economy through Abenomics, leaving behind a mixed economic record.[^cite_note-382][^cite_note-383]

The [senate of the United States](24909346.md) passed resolution 706 a bill to remember Abe.[^cite_note-384]

An exhibition opens in Tokyo about Abe life and includes 100 personal items of the late prime minister and the microphone he was holding on when he was fatally shot.[^cite_note-385]

Abe with the rector of [Turkmen State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkmen_State_University) in 2015

* Hughes, C. W. "Japan's Security Policy in the context of the US-Japan alliance: the emergence of an 'Abe Doctrine'". In: James D.J. Brown and Jeff Kingston (eds.) *Japan's Foreign Relations in Asia* (Palgrave, 2018) pp 49–60.

* Kitaoka, Shinichi. "A 'Proactive Contribution to Peace' and the Right of Collective Self-Defense: The Development of Security Policy in the Abe Administration" *Asia-Pacific Review* (2014) 21(2), pp. 1–18.

* Kolmas, Michal. *National Identity and Japanese Revisionism: Abe Shinzo's vision of a beautiful Japan and its limits* (2020)

* Liff, A. P. "Japan's Defense Policy: Abe the Evolutionary." *The Washington Quarterly,* (2015) 38(2), pp. 79–99.

* Maslow, Sebastian. "A Blueprint for a Strong Japan? Abe Shinzō and Japan's Evolving Security System." *Asian Survey* 55.4 (2015): 739–765. [online](https://www.jstor.org/stable/10.1525/as.2015.55.4.739)

* Oren, Eitan, and Matthew Brummer. "Threat perception, government centralization, and political instrumentality in Abe Shinzo's Japan." *Australian Journal of International Affairs* 74.6 (2020): 721–745.

* Pugliese, Giulio, and Alessio Patalano. "[Diplomatic and security practice under Abe Shinzō: the case for Realpolitik Japan](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1080/10357718.2020.1781790?journalCode=caji20)." *Australian Journal of International Affairs* 74.6 (2020): 615–632.

* Jacob Kovalio . "Abe Shinzō: Ideas and Policies of a Transformative Statesman," at freeandopenindopacific.net. 2022

[^cite_note-Bremmer2022-261]: &#x20;Bremmer, Ian (8 July 2022). ["Shinzo Abe's Formidable Legacy in Japan and the World"](https://time.com/6195116/shinzo-abe-legacy-japan-world/). *Time*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220708181431/https://time.com/6195116/shinzo-abe-legacy-japan-world/) from the original on 8 July 2022. Abe's forceful foreign policy inspired both national self-confidence in Japan and considerable controversy. He was a strong and unapologetic ally of the United States. He went to extraordinary lengths to overcome protectionist pressures within Japan to support the Obama Administration's Trans-Pacific Partnership on trade—and took leadership of the plan when American political leaders of both parties renounced it.

[^cite_note-Rogin2022-262]: &#x20;Rogin, Josh (8 July 2022). ["Abe's legacy is a world better prepared to confront China"](https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2022/07/08/shinzo-abe-japan-china-long-game-quad-asia-pacific-trump-taiwan/). *The Washington Post*. [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220709011640/https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2022/07/08/shinzo-abe-japan-china-long-game-quad-asia-pacific-trump-taiwan/) from the original on 9 July 2022.

[^cite_note-Goodman2022-263]: &#x20;Goodman, Matthew P. (8 July 2022). ["Shinzo Abe's Legacy as Champion of the Global Economic Order"](https://web.archive.org/web/20220708232509/https://www.csis.org/analysis/shinzo-abes-legacy-champion-global-economic-order). *[Center for Strategic and International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Strategic_and_International_Studies)*. Archived from [the original](https://www.csis.org/analysis/shinzo-abes-legacy-champion-global-economic-order) on 8 July 2022. For decades, Japan was essentially a rule taker in the global economy, often assuming a defensive posture in international trade and rarely taking risks to champion new rules and norms. Abe changed all that, as his bold efforts on TPP, quality infrastructure, data governance underscore. At a time when the global economic order is under stress and the United States has pulled back from its traditional role as shaper of global economic rules, Abe's leadership was pivotal.
